People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi Dr. Smith. Well, my husband actually made the appointment, but I guess... well, what brought me in is that things have been just really, really strange at the shop lately. I'm a mechanic, you see, and I've been working on cars for years—went to community college for it and everything—so I know exactly what an engine is supposed to sound like, what a failing alternator sounds like, all of that. But lately, it's not the cars making the noise. 

I keep hearing this man's voice. And I don't mean like a customer wandering into the service bay or someone talking on the radio, I mean it's coming directly from the garage vents. We have these big overhead HVAC vents in the shop, and at first I thought maybe the sound was traveling through the ductwork from the customer waiting room, but it's not. It's right there in the vent. And then... well, sometimes it comes from right under the hood of the cars I'm working on. It is as clear as somebody standing right next to me holding a wrench. 

And he... the voice, I mean... he keeps telling me these awful things. He tells me not to trust my husband, which is just... I don't know why he'd say that, we've been married for a while, but he's so insistent about it, whispering all these warnings. And then he tells me not to touch certain cars in the shop. Like, I'll be assigned a routine brake job on a Honda or something and the voice will just warn me away from it, telling me I absolutely cannot touch that specific car. I try to just put my head down and ignore it, but when a full-grown man is talking right in your ear from behind a radiator, it's impossible to just do your job, you know? It's every day now, and I just can't keep working like this with him talking to me all the time. So that's... yeah, that's why I'm here.
=====================================

But lately, it's not the cars making the noise. \n\nI keep hearing this man's voice. And I don't mean like a customer wandering into the service bay or someone talking on the radio, I mean it's coming directly from the garage vents. We have these big overhead HVAC vents in the shop, and at first I thought maybe the sound was traveling through the ductwork from the customer waiting room, but it's not. It's right there in the vent. And then... well, sometimes it comes from right under the hood of the cars I'm working on. It is as clear as somebody standing right next to me holding a wrench. \n\nAnd he... the voice, I mean... he keeps telling me these awful things. He tells me not to trust my husband, which is just... I don't know why he'd say that, we've been married for a while, but he's so insistent about it, whispering all these warnings. And then he tells me not to touch certain cars in the shop. Like, I'll be assigned a routine brake job on a Honda or something and the voice will just warn me away from it, telling me I absolutely cannot touch that specific car. I try to just put my head down and ignore it, but when a full-grown man is talking right in your ear from behind a radiator, it's impossible to just do your job, you know? It's every day now, and I just can't keep working like this with him talking to me all the time. So that's... yeah, that's why I'm here.\n\n[Last Question]\nAside from hearing this voice, have you been feeling unusually energized, irritable, or perhaps deeply depressed lately?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / automotive_service_technician_or_mechanic / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Elevated_Irritable_Mood\nmanifestation: \"I’ll be flying high like I can fix every car in town and then, if my husband asks why the dishes aren’t done or a tech moves my socket set, I blow up like they’re trying to hold me down.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's funny you ask that, because... honestly, my energy has been all over the place. Like, way off the charts. Sometimes I get to the shop and I just feel this incredible rush, you know? Like I'm absolutely invincible. I'll look at the schedule for the day and think, 'I can knock out every single ticket on this board before lunch.' And I mean, I really feel like I can fix every car in town, all by myself, without even breaking a sweat. It's this buzzing feeling right under my skin, like my blood is carbonated or something, and I just want to go, go, go. 

But then... the flip side of that is just as intense. It's not depression, really, it's more like... I just get so incredibly irritated. Like, zero to sixty in a millisecond. If I'm riding that high and feeling like a super-mechanic, and then my husband comes home and asks something simple, like why the dishes aren't done, or if one of the other techs at the shop just moves my socket set an inch to the left... I just lose it. I completely blow up. It feels like they're doing it on purpose, like they're deliberately trying to slow me down or hold me back when I'm clearly operating on this totally different, higher level. I yelled at my husband for twenty minutes the other night just because he asked if I was going to make dinner, and I was so mad I couldn't even see straight. It's just... it's exhausting, honestly, swinging from feeling like I own the world to wanting to bite someone's head off over a wrench.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, actually. That's... that's the thing that really made my husband push for this appointment. Because, you know, we had that big blow-up about the dishes, and I was just running on pure electricity for a while, but then... well, a few weeks ago, maybe a month ago, things kind of settled down. I wasn't having those huge rushes of energy anymore. I wasn't looking at the board at the shop and thinking I could rebuild ten transmissions by myself in a single afternoon. I was just... normal, I guess? Or at least, quieter. I was actually sleeping through the night, getting a solid eight hours, which was nice because when I'm revved up, I barely sleep at all. I wasn't snapping at people, I wasn't making these grand plans to reorganize the entire parts department... I was just going in, doing my job, and coming home. 

But the voice... the voice never stopped. Even when I was feeling totally level, totally fine, I'd still be under a Chevy Silverado doing a routine oil change, and there he'd be, right in the air filter housing, whispering at me. Telling me not to touch the car, telling me awful things. And... and it wasn't just the voice, either. Even when my mood was completely stable for those couple of weeks, I still had this... this absolute certainty that my husband and my boss at the shop were working together to track my movements. I mean, I was checking the undercarriage of my own car for GPS trackers every morning before work, and I just knew they were monitoring how long I took on every repair ticket to build some kind of case against me. So, yeah, even when I wasn't feeling high or irritable, even when I was just having a quiet, normal few weeks, all that other stuff—the voice in the vents, the tracking—that was all still happening just as much as before. It never really goes away, no matter how my energy is.

I'm a mechanic, you see, and I've been working on cars for years—went to community college for it and everything—so I know exactly what an engine is supposed to sound like, what a failing alternator sounds like, all of that. But lately, it's not the cars making the noise. \n\nI keep hearing this man's voice. And I don't mean like a customer wandering into the service bay or someone talking on the radio, I mean it's coming directly from the garage vents. We have these big overhead HVAC vents in the shop, and at first I thought maybe the sound was traveling through the ductwork from the customer waiting room, but it's not. It's right there in the vent. And then... well, sometimes it comes from right under the hood of the cars I'm working on. It is as clear as somebody standing right next to me holding a wrench. \n\nAnd he... the voice, I mean... he keeps telling me these awful things. He tells me not to trust my husband, which is just... I don't know why he'd say that, we've been married for a while, but he's so insistent about it, whispering all these warnings. And then he tells me not to touch certain cars in the shop. Like, I'll be assigned a routine brake job on a Honda or something and the voice will just warn me away from it, telling me I absolutely cannot touch that specific car. I try to just put my head down and ignore it, but when a full-grown man is talking right in your ear from behind a radiator, it's impossible to just do your job, you know? It's every day now, and I just can't keep working like this with him talking to me all the time. So that's... yeah, that's why I'm here.\n\nDoctor: Aside from hearing this voice, have you been feeling unusually energized, irritable, or perhaps deeply depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Well, it's funny you ask that, because... honestly, my energy has been all over the place. Like, way off the charts. Sometimes I get to the shop and I just feel this incredible rush, you know? Like I'm absolutely invincible. I'll look at the schedule for the day and think, 'I can knock out every single ticket on this board before lunch.' And I mean, I really feel like I can fix every car in town, all by myself, without even breaking a sweat. It's this buzzing feeling right under my skin, like my blood is carbonated or something, and I just want to go, go, go. \n\nBut then... the flip side of that is just as intense. It's not depression, really, it's more like... I just get so incredibly irritated. Like, zero to sixty in a millisecond. If I'm riding that high and feeling like a super-mechanic, and then my husband comes home and asks something simple, like why the dishes aren't done, or if one of the other techs at the shop just moves my socket set an inch to the left... I just lose it. I completely blow up. It feels like they're doing it on purpose, like they're deliberately trying to slow me down or hold me back when I'm clearly operating on this totally different, higher level. I yelled at my husband for twenty minutes the other night just because he asked if I was going to make dinner, and I was so mad I couldn't even see straight. It's just... it's exhausting, honestly, swinging from feeling like I own the world to wanting to bite someone's head off over a wrench.\n\nDoctor: Have there been any periods of at least two weeks where you felt your mood was relatively stable—not feeling that extreme high energy or irritability—but you were still hearing the voice?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. That's... that's the thing that really made my husband push for this appointment. Because, you know, we had that big blow-up about the dishes, and I was just running on pure electricity for a while, but then... well, a few weeks ago, maybe a month ago, things kind of settled down. I wasn't having those huge rushes of energy anymore. I wasn't looking at the board at the shop and thinking I could rebuild ten transmissions by myself in a single afternoon. I was just... normal, I guess? Or at least, quieter. I was actually sleeping through the night, getting a solid eight hours, which was nice because when I'm revved up, I barely sleep at all. I wasn't snapping at people, I wasn't making these grand plans to reorganize the entire parts department... I was just going in, doing my job, and coming home. \n\nBut the voice... the voice never stopped. Even when I was feeling totally level, totally fine, I'd still be under a Chevy Silverado doing a routine oil change, and there he'd be, right in the air filter housing, whispering at me. Telling me not to touch the car, telling me awful things. And... and it wasn't just the voice, either. Even when my mood was completely stable for those couple of weeks, I still had this... this absolute certainty that my husband and my boss at the shop were working together to track my movements. I mean, I was checking the undercarriage of my own car for GPS trackers every morning before work, and I just knew they were monitoring how long I took on every repair ticket to build some kind of case against me. So, yeah, even when I wasn't feeling high or irritable, even when I was just having a quiet, normal few weeks, all that other stuff—the voice in the vents, the tracking—that was all still happening just as much as before. It never really goes away, no matter how my energy is.\n\n[Last Question]\nAside from those times when you felt energized or irritable, have you ever gone through periods where you felt deeply sad, hopeless, or had no interest in doing the things you usually enjoy for at least two weeks?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / automotive_service_technician_or_mechanic / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Negative_Symptoms\nmanifestation: \"There are stretches where I don’t call anyone back, skip the church potlucks I used to help run, and just sit in the house or the bay staring at tools like I can’t make myself care about any of it.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yeah... it’s like a pendulum, I guess, or a battery that just completely dies out. There are these really long stretches, definitely way longer than two weeks, where all that buzzing energy I told you about just... evaporates. And it’s not even just being tired, it’s like... I don't know, like I'm completely hollowed out. I’ll get home and I won't even take my steel-toe boots off for an hour. My phone will ring and ring, and it’s my sister or my friends, and I just stare at the screen. I don't call anyone back. I just can't bring myself to do it, it feels like lifting a thousand pounds just to say hello. 

And I used to be so involved, you know? Like, I used to help run the church potlucks every month. I’d organize the sign-up sheets, make sure we didn't end up with five potato salads and no main dishes, coordinate the folding tables, the whole nine yards. I took a lot of pride in it. But during those down times... I just skip them entirely. People will text me asking where I am, and I just ignore it. I don't care. I literally cannot make myself care about any of it, no matter how hard I try. 

Even at work, which I usually love more than anything, I'll just stand there in the service bay, or I'll be sitting on the couch at the house, just staring at my tools. I’ve got this beautiful master set that I worked so hard to put together over the years, and I’ll just look at the wrenches and the ratchets and feel absolutely nothing. No desire to pick them up, no interest in fixing a single thing. It’s just this heavy, gray feeling where nothing matters, and I just sit there staring, completely blank, wondering how I ever cared about any of it in the first place. It's like the color just drains right out of the world.

high like I can fix every car in town and then, if my husband asks why the dishes aren’t done or a tech moves my socket set, I blow up like they’re trying to hold me down.\n- key: S034\n  name: Increased_Activity\n  description: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.\n  manifestation: When I get going, I’ll stay at the shop after closing, start three repair jobs, plan a car club event, reorganize the parts shelves, and still feel like I need to keep moving.\n- key: S035\n  name: Inflated_Self_Esteem\n  description: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).\n  manifestation: I was sure I was the only one who really understood where the repair business was headed, like if everybody just listened to me I could turn our little shop into the top EV place in the state.\n- key: S036\n  name: Decreased_Sleep\n  description: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).\n  manifestation: For weeks at a time I’d sleep maybe two or three hours, wake up before dawn with my mind already in the garage, and feel like sleeping more would just waste time.\n- key: S037\n  name: Pressured_Speech\n  description: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.\n  manifestation: Once I start explaining my plans or what’s been happening, the words pour out so fast my husband and coworkers tell me to slow down, but stopping feels almost impossible.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: psychotic_symptoms and manic_episode occur concurrently\n  manifestation: During the times I was barely sleeping and racing around the shop with big plans, I was also hearing the voice from the vents and feeling certain the chargers and cameras were being used to watch me.\n- key: additional_requirement_2\n  name: Additional Requirement 2\n  description: manic_episode is present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness\n  manifestation: Most of this past year has been taken over by those high-energy stretches where I talk too fast, take on too much, and feel driven like I can’t slow down.\n- key: additional_requirement_3\n  name: Additional Requirement 3\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode\n  manifestation: Even during a quieter few weeks when I was sleeping more and wasn’t making huge plans, I still heard the garage voice and believed my husband and boss were tracking me.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’ve made mistakes at the shop and was pulled off high-voltage EV work because people were worried I wasn’t safe, and at home the fighting got bad enough that my husband took me for an emergency evaluation.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 years and 3 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about a year and three months, since the arguments at home over my late nights and shop commitments got much worse.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 10 months\n  manifestation: The voices and tracking beliefs have been happening for about ten months now.\n- key: manic_episode_duration\n  name: Manic Episode Duration\n  description: 11 months\n  manifestation: The long stretches of little sleep, racing plans, and nonstop activity have been happening for around eleven months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / automotive_service_technician_or_mechanic / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S020\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Aside from hearing this voice, have you been feeling unusually energized, irritable, or perhaps deeply depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Well, it's funny you ask that, because... honestly, my energy has been all over the place. Like, way off the charts. Sometimes I get to the shop and I just feel this incredible rush, you know? Like I'm absolutely invincible. I'll look at the schedule for the day and think, 'I can knock out every single ticket on this board before lunch.' And I mean, I really feel like I can fix every car in town, all by myself, without even breaking a sweat. It's this buzzing feeling right under my skin, like my blood is carbonated or something, and I just want to go, go, go. \n\nBut then... the flip side of that is just as intense. It's not depression, really, it's more like... I just get so incredibly irritated. Like, zero to sixty in a millisecond. If I'm riding that high and feeling like a super-mechanic, and then my husband comes home and asks something simple, like why the dishes aren't done, or if one of the other techs at the shop just moves my socket set an inch to the left... I just lose it. I completely blow up. It feels like they're doing it on purpose, like they're deliberately trying to slow me down or hold me back when I'm clearly operating on this totally different, higher level. I yelled at my husband for twenty minutes the other night just because he asked if I was going to make dinner, and I was so mad I couldn't even see straight. It's just... it's exhausting, honestly, swinging from feeling like I own the world to wanting to bite someone's head off over a wrench.\n\nDoctor: Have there been any periods of at least two weeks where you felt your mood was relatively stable—not feeling that extreme high energy or irritability—but you were still hearing the voice?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. That's... that's the thing that really made my husband push for this appointment. Because, you know, we had that big blow-up about the dishes, and I was just running on pure electricity for a while, but then... well, a few weeks ago, maybe a month ago, things kind of settled down. I wasn't having those huge rushes of energy anymore. I wasn't looking at the board at the shop and thinking I could rebuild ten transmissions by myself in a single afternoon. I was just... normal, I guess? Or at least, quieter. I was actually sleeping through the night, getting a solid eight hours, which was nice because when I'm revved up, I barely sleep at all. I wasn't snapping at people, I wasn't making these grand plans to reorganize the entire parts department... I was just going in, doing my job, and coming home. \n\nBut the voice... the voice never stopped. Even when I was feeling totally level, totally fine, I'd still be under a Chevy Silverado doing a routine oil change, and there he'd be, right in the air filter housing, whispering at me. Telling me not to touch the car, telling me awful things. And... and it wasn't just the voice, either. Even when my mood was completely stable for those couple of weeks, I still had this... this absolute certainty that my husband and my boss at the shop were working together to track my movements. I mean, I was checking the undercarriage of my own car for GPS trackers every morning before work, and I just knew they were monitoring how long I took on every repair ticket to build some kind of case against me. So, yeah, even when I wasn't feeling high or irritable, even when I was just having a quiet, normal few weeks, all that other stuff—the voice in the vents, the tracking—that was all still happening just as much as before. It never really goes away, no matter how my energy is.\n\nDoctor: Aside from those times when you felt energized or irritable, have you ever gone through periods where you felt deeply sad, hopeless, or had no interest in doing the things you usually enjoy for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah... it’s like a pendulum, I guess, or a battery that just completely dies out. There are these really long stretches, definitely way longer than two weeks, where all that buzzing energy I told you about just... evaporates. And it’s not even just being tired, it’s like... I don't know, like I'm completely hollowed out. I’ll get home and I won't even take my steel-toe boots off for an hour. My phone will ring and ring, and it’s my sister or my friends, and I just stare at the screen. I don't call anyone back. I just can't bring myself to do it, it feels like lifting a thousand pounds just to say hello. \n\nAnd I used to be so involved, you know? Like, I used to help run the church potlucks every month. I’d organize the sign-up sheets, make sure we didn't end up with five potato salads and no main dishes, coordinate the folding tables, the whole nine yards. I took a lot of pride in it. But during those down times... I just skip them entirely. People will text me asking where I am, and I just ignore it. I don't care. I literally cannot make myself care about any of it, no matter how hard I try. \n\nEven at work, which I usually love more than anything, I'll just stand there in the service bay, or I'll be sitting on the couch at the house, just staring at my tools. I’ve got this beautiful master set that I worked so hard to put together over the years, and I’ll just look at the wrenches and the ratchets and feel absolutely nothing. No desire to pick them up, no interest in fixing a single thing. It’s just this heavy, gray feeling where nothing matters, and I just sit there staring, completely blank, wondering how I ever cared about any of it in the first place. It's like the color just drains right out of the world.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring those times when you felt hollow and uninterested in your usual activities, did you also experience physical symptoms like significant changes in your appetite, sleep patterns, or feeling physically slowed down or agitated?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

Well, I mean... it's hard to say for sure about all of that. Like, I don't really know if I was physically slowed down or agitated or anything like that. I just remember feeling so incredibly heavy, like my limbs were made of lead, you know? But I wouldn't say I was pacing around or feeling physically restless like I do when I'm all revved up. It was more just that total lack of energy, that feeling of being completely drained. 

And as for my appetite and sleep... I'm really not sure. I mean, my husband does most of the cooking anyway, so I'd just eat whatever he put in front of me, or maybe I wouldn't finish it, but I don't remember any huge changes. I wasn't, like, binge-eating or starving myself, at least not that I noticed. And my sleep... well, my sleep is always kind of a mess anyway, between the voice and everything else. When I'm in those dark periods, I guess I spend a lot of time in bed, just lying there staring at the ceiling because getting up feels impossible. But am I actually sleeping more? Or am I waking up super early? I honestly couldn't tell you. It all just blurs together into this one long, gray stretch of time where nothing really stands out. I just know I feel completely empty, but the physical stuff... I just haven't really paid attention to it, to be honest. It's the emptiness that really takes over everything else.

It's just... it's exhausting, honestly, swinging from feeling like I own the world to wanting to bite someone's head off over a wrench.\n\nDoctor: Have there been any periods of at least two weeks where you felt your mood was relatively stable—not feeling that extreme high energy or irritability—but you were still hearing the voice?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. It's like the color just drains right out of the world.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you felt hollow and uninterested in your usual activities, did you also experience physical symptoms like significant changes in your appetite, sleep patterns, or feeling physically slowed down or agitated?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... it's hard to say for sure about all of that. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yeah, like I was trying to explain earlier... it's exactly like that. There was that period, maybe a month or so ago, where I wasn't up and I wasn't down. I wasn't running on pure electricity and I wasn't feeling all hollowed out and heavy like lead either. It was just... a quieter few weeks. And I remember it specifically because it felt so strange to just be average, you know? I was actually sleeping more, getting a real night's sleep, waking up without feeling like I needed to conquer the world or hide from it. I wasn't making those huge plans to overhaul the parts department or rebuild a dozen transmissions. I was just waking up, having a cup of coffee, packing my lunch, and driving to the shop. Just totally, completely stable. 

But the thing is... the other stuff? It doesn't care if I'm stable. It really doesn't. Because even during those quiet weeks, even when I felt totally fine in my own skin, I'd get to work, pull a car into the service bay, and the second I'd pop the hood, I still heard that garage voice. Clear as day. Just whispering at me from the engine blocks or the air filters, telling me all those awful things, telling me not to touch the cars. 

And the whole time, even though my mood was totally level, I still completely believed that my husband and my boss were tracking me. I mean, I’d be having a perfectly normal, calm morning, but I’d still be out there crawling under my car in the driveway, freezing my knees off on the concrete, looking for a GPS tracker on the chassis before I’d even leave the house. I just knew they were working together to monitor my repair times and build a case against me. So, yeah, even when everything else is quiet and I'm sleeping great and not making any wild plans, the voice and the tracking... they just keep right on going. It's like they're on a totally different circuit than my mood, they just never shut off.

That's... that's the thing that really made my husband push for this appointment. So, yeah, even when I wasn't feeling high or irritable, even when I was just having a quiet, normal few weeks, all that other stuff—the voice in the vents, the tracking—that was all still happening just as much as before. It never really goes away, no matter how my energy is.\n\nDoctor: Aside from those times when you felt energized or irritable, have you ever gone through periods where you felt deeply sad, hopeless, or had no interest in doing the things you usually enjoy for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah... it’s like a pendulum, I guess, or a battery that just completely dies out. Gosh, how long? I mean, time is just... it's such a weird concept when you're in the middle of all of this, you know? It really is. Because when I’m in one of those states, the clock just stops making any sense whatsoever. 

When I get all revved up, when that electricity is just coursing through my veins and I'm tearing apart a transmission or trying to reorganize the entire parts inventory at the shop by serial number instead of alphabetical order—which, by the way, makes way more sense if you really think about how the manufacturers update their catalogs, but nobody listens to me about that—time just completely evaporates. I could be running on that high for... well, I honestly don't even know. Is it a few days? Is it a couple of weeks? It feels like one continuous, never-ending day because I'm barely sleeping, or I'm just taking these tiny little ten-minute catnaps in the breakroom chair before I'm back out on the floor. So I completely lose track of the calendar. Tuesday feels like Saturday, and I couldn't tell you how long the whole thing lasted if my life depended on it.

And then the hollow periods... oh man, those are even harder to put a number on. Because when you're just sitting there, like I was telling you, staring at your steel-toe boots and not even bothering to unlace them, a single afternoon can feel like an entire decade. I'll sit on the couch and watch the sun go down through the living room window, and watching the shadows move across the floor feels like it takes a century. So when you ask how long those stretches last, I genuinely couldn't give you a straight answer. It's just a blur. My husband might know, maybe? He's the one who has to watch me sit there like a lump. But to me, it just feels like this endless gray fog. It could be three weeks, it could be two months, it could be a whole season. I just know it feels like forever when I'm in it, but looking back, it's all just a wash of nothingness. 

I really wish I had kept a calendar or a journal or something so I could give you an exact number of days, but honestly, who has the energy to write in a diary when you can't even convince yourself to pick up a wrench, right? So, yeah, I really have no idea how long they actually last on a real-world clock. It's impossible to say.

Or am I waking up super early? I honestly couldn't tell you. It all just blurs together into this one long, gray stretch of time where nothing really stands out. I just know I feel completely empty, but the physical stuff... I just haven't really paid attention to it, to be honest. It's the emptiness that really takes over everything else.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced the voice and the feelings of being tracked during a period of time when your mood was completely stable—meaning you were not feeling that high energy, nor the hollow, empty, or depressed feelings?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, like I was trying to explain earlier... it's exactly like that. There was that period, maybe a month or so ago, where I wasn't up and I wasn't down. I wasn't running on pure electricity and I wasn't feeling all hollowed out and heavy like lead either. It was just... a quieter few weeks. And I remember it specifically because it felt so strange to just be average, you know? I was actually sleeping more, getting a real night's sleep, waking up without feeling like I needed to conquer the world or hide from it. I wasn't making those huge plans to overhaul the parts department or rebuild a dozen transmissions. I was just waking up, having a cup of coffee, packing my lunch, and driving to the shop. Just totally, completely stable. \n\nBut the thing is... the other stuff? It doesn't care if I'm stable. It really doesn't. So, yeah, even when everything else is quiet and I'm sleeping great and not making any wild plans, the voice and the tracking... they just keep right on going. It's like they're on a totally different circuit than my mood, they just never shut off.\n\nDoctor: Looking back at the times you felt either the extreme high energy or the deep emptiness, how long would you say those periods typically lasted?\n\nPatient: Gosh, how long? I could be running on that high for... well, I honestly don't even know. Is it a few days? Is it a couple of weeks? Well, yeah... I mean, when I get into that state, it definitely lasts longer than a week, and it... it definitely messes things up, even though at the time I think I'm doing great. Because I feel like I'm moving faster and better than everyone else, right? But the reality is, I'm just moving *too* fast. 

Like, I started making mistakes at the shop. Stupid mistakes, things I would normally never, ever do. We get a lot of electric vehicles in now, and I was working on one of those—the high-voltage EV work where you really, really have to follow the safety protocols, like the lockout-tagout procedures and wearing the right insulated gloves, because if you touch the wrong orange cable, that voltage can literally stop your heart. And I was just rushing, my brain was ten steps ahead of my hands, and people started noticing. They got really worried that I wasn't safe, that I was going to get myself or someone else electrocuted, so management actually pulled me off the high-voltage EV work completely. They just wouldn't let me near those cars anymore, which was really frustrating because I know how to do it, I just couldn't slow down enough to show them.

And it wasn't just at work, either. I'd take all that buzzing, irritable energy home with me, and I was just impossible to be around. Every little thing my husband did would just set me off. If he asked me a simple question or didn't understand why I was awake at 3 AM pacing the kitchen, I would just explode on him. The fighting got so incredibly bad... just these massive, screaming blowouts over absolutely nothing because I couldn't shut that electricity off and I was just so easily agitated by everything he did. It got to the point where it was so bad, and I was so wound up for days and days on end, that my husband actually made me get in the car and took me in for an emergency evaluation. He just didn't know what else to do with me, because I was completely out of control and it was just tearing everything apart at home and at the shop. So, yeah, it definitely interferes with things.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, no, it didn't stop. It never stops. I really wish it did, like maybe the voice would take a little vacation just because my brain is already running a million miles a minute, but no. It's almost like... well, it’s like it all just layers right on top of each other, you know? 

During those times when I was barely sleeping—when I was just completely wired and racing around the shop with all those big plans, like that whole parts inventory overhaul I was trying to explain to you earlier—I was absolutely still dealing with the voices and the tracking. It didn't go away at all. If anything, because I was moving so fast, I was noticing it in even more places. Like, I’d be rushing through a repair, and instead of just hearing the voice coming from the air filters or the engine blocks, I'd hear it coming right out of the overhead AC vents in the service bays. Just this constant, terrible whispering drifting down from the ceiling grates while I was trying to turn a wrench. 

And the tracking? Oh my gosh, it was everywhere. Because when I'm in that high-energy state, I'm just so incredibly hyper-aware of every single detail in my surroundings. It wasn't just me crawling under my car in the driveway looking for GPS trackers anymore. I'd be at the shop, and I was dead certain that the battery chargers—especially those big, heavy-duty diagnostic chargers we hook up to the bays—were somehow rigged to monitor my movements and pull data on me. And the security cameras! We have these dome cameras pointing at the work areas, and management always says they're just for liability, like if a customer claims we scratched their fender or something. But I knew. I was absolutely certain they were using those cameras specifically to watch me. I felt it in my bones. 

I'd be standing there, running on maybe two hours of sleep, gripping a tool so tight my knuckles were white, just glaring up at that camera lens because I knew they were using it to record my every move and build their case against me. So, yeah, even when I was completely revved up and buzzing out of my skin, the voice from the vents and the cameras and the chargers... it was all still happening right alongside the energy. And it was the exact same during the empty, hollow times, too. I'd just sit there on the couch, feeling heavy as lead, and I'd still hear it, still feel like I was being watched. It just never gives me a break, no matter what my mood is actually doing.

It's nice to meet you. Could you tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi Dr. Smith. Well, my husband actually made the appointment, but I guess... well, what brought me in is that things have been just really, really strange at the shop lately. I'm a mechanic, you see, and I've been working on cars for years—went to community college for it and everything—so I know exactly what an engine is supposed to sound like, what a failing alternator sounds like, all of that. But lately, it's not the cars making the noise. \n\nI keep hearing this man's voice. And I don't mean like a customer wandering into the service bay or someone talking on the radio, I mean it's coming directly from the garage vents. We have these big overhead HVAC vents in the shop, and at first I thought maybe the sound was traveling through the ductwork from the customer waiting room, but it's not. It's right there in the vent. And then... well, sometimes it comes from right under the hood of the cars I'm working on. It is as clear as somebody standing right next to me holding a wrench. \n\nAnd he... the voice, I mean... he keeps telling me these awful things. He tells me not to trust my husband, which is just... I don't know why he'd say that, we've been married for a while, but he's so insistent about it, whispering all these warnings. And then he tells me not to touch certain cars in the shop. Like, I'll be assigned a routine brake job on a Honda or something and the voice will just warn me away from it, telling me I absolutely cannot touch that specific car. I try to just put my head down and ignore it, but when a full-grown man is talking right in your ear from behind a radiator, it's impossible to just do your job, you know? It's every day now, and I just can't keep working like this with him talking to me all the time. So that's... yeah, that's why I'm here.\n\nDoctor: Aside from hearing this voice, have you been feeling unusually energized, irritable, or perhaps deeply depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Well, it's funny you ask that, because... honestly, my energy has been all over the place. Like, way off the charts. Sometimes I get to the shop and I just feel this incredible rush, you know? Like I'm absolutely invincible. I'll look at the schedule for the day and think, 'I can knock out every single ticket on this board before lunch.' And I mean, I really feel like I can fix every car in town, all by myself, without even breaking a sweat. It's this buzzing feeling right under my skin, like my blood is carbonated or something, and I just want to go, go, go. \n\nBut then... the flip side of that is just as intense. It's not depression, really, it's more like... I just get so incredibly irritated. Like, zero to sixty in a millisecond. If I'm riding that high and feeling like a super-mechanic, and then my husband comes home and asks something simple, like why the dishes aren't done, or if one of the other techs at the shop just moves my socket set an inch to the left... I just lose it. I completely blow up. It feels like they're doing it on purpose, like they're deliberately trying to slow me down or hold me back when I'm clearly operating on this totally different, higher level. I yelled at my husband for twenty minutes the other night just because he asked if I was going to make dinner, and I was so mad I couldn't even see straight. It's just... it's exhausting, honestly, swinging from feeling like I own the world to wanting to bite someone's head off over a wrench.\n\nDoctor: Have there been any periods of at least two weeks where you felt your mood was relatively stable—not feeling that extreme high energy or irritability—but you were still hearing the voice?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. That's... that's the thing that really made my husband push for this appointment. Because, you know, we had that big blow-up about the dishes, and I was just running on pure electricity for a while, but then... well, a few weeks ago, maybe a month ago, things kind of settled down. I wasn't having those huge rushes of energy anymore. I wasn't looking at the board at the shop and thinking I could rebuild ten transmissions by myself in a single afternoon. I was just... normal, I guess? Or at least, quieter. I was actually sleeping through the night, getting a solid eight hours, which was nice because when I'm revved up, I barely sleep at all. I wasn't snapping at people, I wasn't making these grand plans to reorganize the entire parts department... I was just going in, doing my job, and coming home. \n\nBut the voice... the voice never stopped. Even when I was feeling totally level, totally fine, I'd still be under a Chevy Silverado doing a routine oil change, and there he'd be, right in the air filter housing, whispering at me. Telling me not to touch the car, telling me awful things. And... and it wasn't just the voice, either. Even when my mood was completely stable for those couple of weeks, I still had this... this absolute certainty that my husband and my boss at the shop were working together to track my movements. I mean, I was checking the undercarriage of my own car for GPS trackers every morning before work, and I just knew they were monitoring how long I took on every repair ticket to build some kind of case against me. So, yeah, even when I wasn't feeling high or irritable, even when I was just having a quiet, normal few weeks, all that other stuff—the voice in the vents, the tracking—that was all still happening just as much as before. It never really goes away, no matter how my energy is.\n\nDoctor: Aside from those times when you felt energized or irritable, have you ever gone through periods where you felt deeply sad, hopeless, or had no interest in doing the things you usually enjoy for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah... it’s like a pendulum, I guess, or a battery that just completely dies out. There are these really long stretches, definitely way longer than two weeks, where all that buzzing energy I told you about just... evaporates. And it’s not even just being tired, it’s like... I don't know, like I'm completely hollowed out. I’ll get home and I won't even take my steel-toe boots off for an hour. My phone will ring and ring, and it’s my sister or my friends, and I just stare at the screen. I don't call anyone back. I just can't bring myself to do it, it feels like lifting a thousand pounds just to say hello. \n\nAnd I used to be so involved, you know? Like, I used to help run the church potlucks every month. I’d organize the sign-up sheets, make sure we didn't end up with five potato salads and no main dishes, coordinate the folding tables, the whole nine yards. I took a lot of pride in it. But during those down times... I just skip them entirely. People will text me asking where I am, and I just ignore it. I don't care. I literally cannot make myself care about any of it, no matter how hard I try. \n\nEven at work, which I usually love more than anything, I'll just stand there in the service bay, or I'll be sitting on the couch at the house, just staring at my tools. I’ve got this beautiful master set that I worked so hard to put together over the years, and I’ll just look at the wrenches and the ratchets and feel absolutely nothing. No desire to pick them up, no interest in fixing a single thing. It’s just this heavy, gray feeling where nothing matters, and I just sit there staring, completely blank, wondering how I ever cared about any of it in the first place. It's like the color just drains right out of the world.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you felt hollow and uninterested in your usual activities, did you also experience physical symptoms like significant changes in your appetite, sleep patterns, or feeling physically slowed down or agitated?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... it's hard to say for sure about all of that. Like, I don't really know if I was physically slowed down or agitated or anything like that. I just remember feeling so incredibly heavy, like my limbs were made of lead, you know? But I wouldn't say I was pacing around or feeling physically restless like I do when I'm all revved up. It was more just that total lack of energy, that feeling of being completely drained. \n\nAnd as for my appetite and sleep... I'm really not sure. I mean, my husband does most of the cooking anyway, so I'd just eat whatever he put in front of me, or maybe I wouldn't finish it, but I don't remember any huge changes. I wasn't, like, binge-eating or starving myself, at least not that I noticed. And my sleep... well, my sleep is always kind of a mess anyway, between the voice and everything else. When I'm in those dark periods, I guess I spend a lot of time in bed, just lying there staring at the ceiling because getting up feels impossible. But am I actually sleeping more? Or am I waking up super early? I honestly couldn't tell you. It all just blurs together into this one long, gray stretch of time where nothing really stands out. I just know I feel completely empty, but the physical stuff... I just haven't really paid attention to it, to be honest. It's the emptiness that really takes over everything else.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced the voice and the feelings of being tracked during a period of time when your mood was completely stable—meaning you were not feeling that high energy, nor the hollow, empty, or depressed feelings?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, like I was trying to explain earlier... it's exactly like that. There was that period, maybe a month or so ago, where I wasn't up and I wasn't down. I wasn't running on pure electricity and I wasn't feeling all hollowed out and heavy like lead either. It was just... a quieter few weeks. And I remember it specifically because it felt so strange to just be average, you know? I was actually sleeping more, getting a real night's sleep, waking up without feeling like I needed to conquer the world or hide from it. I wasn't making those huge plans to overhaul the parts department or rebuild a dozen transmissions. I was just waking up, having a cup of coffee, packing my lunch, and driving to the shop. Just totally, completely stable. \n\nBut the thing is... the other stuff? It doesn't care if I'm stable. It really doesn't. So, yeah, even when everything else is quiet and I'm sleeping great and not making any wild plans, the voice and the tracking... they just keep right on going. It's like they're on a totally different circuit than my mood, they just never shut off.\n\nDoctor: Looking back at the times you felt either the extreme high energy or the deep emptiness, how long would you say those periods typically lasted?\n\nPatient: Gosh, how long? I mean, time is just... it's such a weird concept when you're in the middle of all of this, you know? It really is. Because when I’m in one of those states, the clock just stops making any sense whatsoever. \n\nWhen I get all revved up, when that electricity is just coursing through my veins and I'm tearing apart a transmission or trying to reorganize the entire parts inventory at the shop by serial number instead of alphabetical order—which, by the way, makes way more sense if you really think about how the manufacturers update their catalogs, but nobody listens to me about that—time just completely evaporates. I could be running on that high for... well, I honestly don't even know. Is it a few days? Is it a couple of weeks? It feels like one continuous, never-ending day because I'm barely sleeping, or I'm just taking these tiny little ten-minute catnaps in the breakroom chair before I'm back out on the floor. So I completely lose track of the calendar. Tuesday feels like Saturday, and I couldn't tell you how long the whole thing lasted if my life depended on it.\n\nAnd then the hollow periods... oh man, those are even harder to put a number on. Because when you're just sitting there, like I was telling you, staring at your steel-toe boots and not even bothering to unlace them, a single afternoon can feel like an entire decade. I'll sit on the couch and watch the sun go down through the living room window, and watching the shadows move across the floor feels like it takes a century. So when you ask how long those stretches last, I genuinely couldn't give you a straight answer. It's just a blur. My husband might know, maybe? He's the one who has to watch me sit there like a lump. But to me, it just feels like this endless gray fog. It could be three weeks, it could be two months, it could be a whole season. I just know it feels like forever when I'm in it, but looking back, it's all just a wash of nothingness. \n\nI really wish I had kept a calendar or a journal or something so I could give you an exact number of days, but honestly, who has the energy to write in a diary when you can't even convince yourself to pick up a wrench, right? So, yeah, I really have no idea how long they actually last on a real-world clock. It's impossible to say.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you felt that extreme 'buzzing' energy and felt like you could fix every car in town, did you ever experience a period lasting at least one full week where you were so energized or irritable that it significantly interfered with your work or your ability to function, or resulted in you needing to be hospitalized?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah... I mean, when I get into that state, it definitely lasts longer than a week, and it... it definitely messes things up, even though at the time I think I'm doing great. Because I feel like I'm moving faster and better than everyone else, right? But the reality is, I'm just moving *too* fast. \n\nLike, I started making mistakes at the shop. Stupid mistakes, things I would normally never, ever do. We get a lot of electric vehicles in now, and I was working on one of those—the high-voltage EV work where you really, really have to follow the safety protocols, like the lockout-tagout procedures and wearing the right insulated gloves, because if you touch the wrong orange cable, that voltage can literally stop your heart. And I was just rushing, my brain was ten steps ahead of my hands, and people started noticing. They got really worried that I wasn't safe, that I was going to get myself or someone else electrocuted, so management actually pulled me off the high-voltage EV work completely. They just wouldn't let me near those cars anymore, which was really frustrating because I know how to do it, I just couldn't slow down enough to show them.\n\nAnd it wasn't just at work, either. I'd take all that buzzing, irritable energy home with me, and I was just impossible to be around. Every little thing my husband did would just set me off. If he asked me a simple question or didn't understand why I was awake at 3 AM pacing the kitchen, I would just explode on him. The fighting got so incredibly bad... just these massive, screaming blowouts over absolutely nothing because I couldn't shut that electricity off and I was just so easily agitated by everything he did. It got to the point where it was so bad, and I was so wound up for days and days on end, that my husband actually made me get in the car and took me in for an emergency evaluation. He just didn't know what else to do with me, because I was completely out of control and it was just tearing everything apart at home and at the shop. So, yeah, it definitely interferes with things.\n\nDoctor: When you look back at those times when you were feeling either the extreme high energy or the deep, hollow emptiness, were you also hearing the voice and feeling like you were being tracked during those specific times, or did those things only happen when your mood was stable?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it didn't stop. It never stops. I really wish it did, like maybe the voice would take a little vacation just because my brain is already running a million miles a minute, but no. It's almost like... well, it’s like it all just layers right on top of each other, you know? \n\nDuring those times when I was barely sleeping—when I was just completely wired and racing around the shop with all those big plans, like that whole parts inventory overhaul I was trying to explain to you earlier—I was absolutely still dealing with the voices and the tracking. It didn't go away at all. If anything, because I was moving so fast, I was noticing it in even more places. Like, I’d be rushing through a repair, and instead of just hearing the voice coming from the air filters or the engine blocks, I'd hear it coming right out of the overhead AC vents in the service bays. Just this constant, terrible whispering drifting down from the ceiling grates while I was trying to turn a wrench. \n\nAnd the tracking? Oh my gosh, it was everywhere. Because when I'm in that high-energy state, I'm just so incredibly hyper-aware of every single detail in my surroundings. It wasn't just me crawling under my car in the driveway looking for GPS trackers anymore. I'd be at the shop, and I was dead certain that the battery chargers—especially those big, heavy-duty diagnostic chargers we hook up to the bays—were somehow rigged to monitor my movements and pull data on me. And the security cameras! We have these dome cameras pointing at the work areas, and management always says they're just for liability, like if a customer claims we scratched their fender or something. But I knew. I was absolutely certain they were using those cameras specifically to watch me. I felt it in my bones. \n\nI'd be standing there, running on maybe two hours of sleep, gripping a tool so tight my knuckles were white, just glaring up at that camera lens because I knew they were using it to record my every move and build their case against me. So, yeah, even when I was completely revved up and buzzing out of my skin, the voice from the vents and the cameras and the chargers... it was all still happening right alongside the energy. And it was the exact same during the empty, hollow times, too. I'd just sit there on the couch, feeling heavy as lead, and I'd still hear it, still feel like I was being watched. It just never gives me a break, no matter what my mood is actually doing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah, you know... I actually was just thinking about that. Because it’s confusing, right? You’d think if the energy levels out and the heavy feeling lifts, everything else would just kind of pack up and go home too. But... no, it doesn't. 

There was this one stretch, I want to say it was maybe a month, or at least three or four solid weeks, where things were actually... quiet? Well, quiet in my body, anyway. I wasn't buzzing, I wasn't snapping at anyone. I was actually sleeping a normal amount, like a solid seven or eight hours a night, which is practically a miracle for me when you consider how I usually am. And I wasn't making any of those huge, massive plans to reorganize the parts department or rewrite the safety manuals. I was just going in, doing standard brake jobs and tire rotations—the really mundane, everyday stuff—and then coming home and having a normal dinner. I wasn't depressed, either. I wasn't hollow or staring at my boots. I was just... fine. Mood-wise, I was completely level.

But the other stuff? That didn't take a break at all. Even during those quieter few weeks when I was sleeping more and just kind of living a normal life, I still heard that voice in the garage. It would echo right off the concrete floors while I was standing under the hydraulic lift, just talking and talking. 

And the tracking... honestly, because my mood was so stable and I wasn't rushing around, I felt like I could see it all even more clearly. I firmly believed that my husband and my boss were in on it together, tracking my every move. I was absolutely certain of it. Like, my boss would ask me a weirdly specific question about what time I left the house that morning, and then later my husband would ask if I took the highway or the backroads home, and I just knew—I *knew*—they were comparing my location data. I figured they must have synced my phone to some sort of fleet management software at the shop. I'd be laying in bed at night, totally calm, not agitated or wired at all, just staring at the ceiling and knowing for a fact that my husband was reporting my movements to my manager. 

So, yeah... even when my mood was completely fine and steady for weeks on end, the voice and the tracking were still right there, every single day. It just never really leaves me alone.

    "additional_requirements": [
      "Psychotic symptoms present for at least 2 weeks in the absence of a major mood episode",
      "Symptoms that meet criteria for a major mood episode are present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness"
    ]
  }
}
